Wave Forms Already in the Patched Form: The Lines Moved Out of the Inventory

This file holds the lines that wave-forms.md no longer carries. Every row of that inventory whose exponentials are all of the patched shape has been moved here, and the line is quoted verbatim from its article. Nothing was retyped and nothing was normalised.

The shape is the selection. An exponential belongs here when its argument is $i\theta_{\mathrm{xxx}}$, up to an overall sign, an integer coefficient, and the choice between $\exp(\,\cdot\,)$ and $e^{\,\cdot\,}$. So $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$, $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{pwp}})$, $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ and $\exp(2i\theta_{\mathrm{mat}})$ are in. The form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pwp}}/2)$ is out, because the exponent is a fraction of the angle and not the angle. The corpus writes that half-angle shape in the rotor, retardance and chiral sections, and none of those sites is quoted here.

Each line is quoted exactly, with one change: a bar inside the line is escaped as \| so the bar does not end the table cell. The article column links to the file, the form column shows the exponential or exponentials the line carries, and the fourth column is the line itself. The fifth column repeats what the inventory said the exponent carries in that article, and it is written once per article in a family, on the first of its rows.

A line that carries two angles of one family is quoted once, and its form column lists both. A quoted line may hold other exponentials as well, and those are not named in the form column. The tables hold 1092 lines in 230 files, carrying 1372 exponentials whose argument is the angle alone. A further 113 lines in 46 files multiply the angle by a factor; they carry 137 such exponentials and stand in their own section.

2 Addition of Angular Momenta and Clebsch–Gordan Coefficients in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ The transformation is not unique as a matrix. Only the coupled idempotents are canonical: the decomposition $\mathbb{C}^4=\mathrm{sym}^2\oplus\Lambda^2$ is canonical, and within each summand the diagonalisation of $\tilde S_{\Psi,3}$ is canonical once the axis is fixed. What remains free is the phase of each coupled state, and it is fixed below by the Condon–Shortley convention. This is the same freedom that appears for any multiplet in the framework: a state is defined up to a central phase $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})e_t$, and the relative phases of a multiplet are a convention.
3 Addition of Angular Momenta and Clebsch–Gordan Coefficients in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ and the signs of the remaining entries follow. 4 Algebra and Element Conventions $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ One structural fact governs the whole series, so it is worth isolating. The scalar unit $i$ is central: it commutes with every element of $\mathbb{B}$, because it belongs to the $\mathbb{C}$ factor of the tensor product, while the quaternion units belong to the $\mathbb{H}$ factor. Multiplication by a central phase $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ therefore commutes with every operator constructed from the algebra — in particular with the biquaternionic gradient $\tilde\nabla_\Phi$ — and this is why the central phase is the algebra's natural continuous symmetry. The articles on Noether's theorem and the gauge principle rest on it.
5 Bogoliubov Transformations in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ Numerical check. With $\mathsf{M}_2$ the $2\times2$ complex representation, sample $\alpha=\cos\vartheta_{\mathrm{par}}$, $\beta=\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})\sin\vartheta_{\mathrm{par}}$ on $20000$ random $(\vartheta_{\mathrm{par}},\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$. The first anticommutator equals $e_t$ for all samples, with a maximum residual of $4.4\times10^{-16}$; the second equals $2\alpha\beta e_t$, a central element (off-diagonal part identically zero), with magnitude $\|2\alpha\beta\|=\|\sin 2\vartheta_{\mathrm{par}}\|\le1$, maximal at $\vartheta_{\mathrm{par}}=\pi/4$ where $\|\alpha\|=\|\beta\|=1/\sqrt2$ and $\|2\alpha\beta\|=1$. The exact cases $\beta=0$ and $\alpha=0$ give $\{\vec b,\vec b\}=0$ to machine precision. The second anticommutator is thus the sole obstruction, and its maximum violation is the maximal squeezing amplitude.

14 Nonlinear Maxwell Equations in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$, $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ because $\sin\theta_{\mathrm{pha}} - i\cos\theta_{\mathrm{pha}} = -i\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$. 15 Nonlinear Maxwell Equations in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ where the modulus is the ordinary Coulomb field and the phase is a function of time alone. It solves the nonlinear equation to $1.2 \times 10^{-12}$ over $100$ random points and phases, and it does so for any phase function, not only a linear one: with $\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}(r'_t) = \cos(\omega r'_t/c)$ the residual was $1.7 \times 10^{-13}$. The derivation in the paper says why. Writing $f = s\,\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ as its (12.5), the first of its (12.3) gives $\mathrm{Re}\{f^{*}\partial/\partial r'_tf\} = s\,\partial/\partial r'_ts = 0$, so the modulus is static; the second reduces, through its (12.4) and (12.6), to $\theta_{\mathrm{pha},r} = 0$ and $s = \alpha/r^3$. The phase is therefore left completely free — the equations fix the spatial profile and the static modulus, never $\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}(r'_t)$ — and the paper's reading, that the charge is complex and the phase measures how much of it is electric and how much magnetic, is a reading of that freedom rather than a consequence of it. The corpus records the reading as the paper's, and notes that Exercise: Duality Rotation and the Riemann–Silberstein Vector supplies exactly the internal rotation that a complex amplitude implements, so the identification of the phase with the electric–magnetic mix is the corpus's duality rotation and is not new to the paper.

18 Norm and Pairing Conventions $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ The polar word is $\tilde Q=r\,e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}\tilde U_{\Psi,\mathrm b}\tilde u_{\mathrm r}$ on the complement of the zero-divisor cone. 22 Pancharatnam's Phase and the Polarization Sphere in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha},k})$ The residual phase is a property of closed circuits only. If the representatives are changed by the real re-phasing angle $\theta_{\mathrm{pha},k}$, $\|J_\eta\rangle\to \exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha},k})\|J_\eta\rangle$, the overlaps change by $\exp(i(\xi_{k+1}-\xi_k))$, and the product around a closed sequence is unchanged because the phases telescope. Hence the Pancharatnam phase of a circuit is gauge invariant, while the phase $\arg\langle A\|B\rangle$ of a pair is not: the physical content of a pair of states is the interference visibility $\|\langle A\|B\rangle\|$, and the physical content of a circuit is the closed-loop phase. This is the same distinction that the symplectic article draws between the potential and the symplectic form: the local object is gauge dependent, the loop integral is not.

24 POVMs and the Positive Cone in Biquaternionic Form $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ The effect is the squared strength conjugated by the backaction. The effect therefore sees the strength but not the backaction: $\tilde M_{\mathrm{kraus},y}$ and $\tilde W_{\Psi}\tilde M_{\mathrm{kraus},y}$ with $\tilde W_{\Psi}$ $*$-unitary have the same effect and different polar factors.

27 Quantum Gates and Circuits in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ A caution about representatives. A gate is a coset $\tilde Q\cdot \mathrm{U}(1)$; the representative can be chosen Hermitian only when the gate is an involution. Membership of the representative in $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$ or in $\mathbb{B}_{H}$ is therefore a property of the chosen representative, not an invariant of the gate. The one representative-independent statement is the factorisation $\tilde Q = \exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})\tilde u_{\mathrm r}$: every gate is a central phase times a rotation, and no gate is anything else.
28 Quantum Gates and Circuits in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ Second, because the map $\tilde Q\mapstoΞ_{\tilde Q}$ has kernel $\mathrm{U}(1)$, the product law holds at the level of gates but the algebraic identity of representatives may fail by a central phase. If $\tilde Q_{\mathrm{gate},2}\tilde Q_{\mathrm{gate},1} = \exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})\tilde Q_{\mathrm{gate},3}$, then $Ξ_{\tilde Q_{\mathrm{gate},2}}\circΞ_{\tilde Q_{\mathrm{gate},1}} = Ξ_{\tilde Q_{\mathrm{gate},3}}$ nevertheless. Circuit identities in the framework are therefore identities up to a central phase, and the phase is unobservable.
29 Quantum Gates and Circuits in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ The standard single-qubit gate set has explicit representatives: the Pauli gates are $\tilde Q_x=i\vec e_x$, $\tilde Q_y=i\vec e_y$, $\tilde Q_z=i\vec e_z$; the Hadamard is $\tilde Q_{\mathrm{Had}}=\tfrac{i}{\sqrt2}(\vec e_x+\vec e_z)$; the phase and $T$ gates are the rotations $\tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}(e_t+\vec e_z)$ and $\cos\tfrac{\pi}{8}e_t+\sin\tfrac{\pi}{8}\vec e_z$; a general gate is $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})(\cos\tfrac{\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}{2}e_t+\sin\tfrac{\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}{2}\vec u_{\mathrm r})$. Apart from the identity gate, the Hermitian gates are the $\pi$-rotations $\pm i\vec u_{\mathrm r}$; the Pauli and Hadamard gates are of this type, the phase and $T$ gates are not.

48 Subluminal and Superluminal Electromagnetic Waves in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ The condition $-i\tilde K_{\Phi}f=0$ is four independent complex conditions on the six complex components of $f$. On a null $k$ its kernel is two-dimensional, the two polarizations, and on the whole kernel $f^2=0$: the plane wave has null invariants. The step is not a cancellation, because a null $-i\tilde K_{\Phi}$ has no inverse; it is the rank of left multiplication by a null element. Checked on the six components for several null $k$, the kernel is two-dimensional and $f^2=0$ on it. The invariant $\tilde F^2=f^2\exp(2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ vanishes with it.

50 The Aharonov–Casher Effect in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha},AC})$ where the factor $i$ makes the exponent anti-Hermitian: the connection $\mathcal{A}_{AC}$ is $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$-valued, and it is $i\mathcal{A}_{AC}$ that generates a $*$-unitary transport, exactly as $-\frac{i\,H}{\hbar\,c}$ does for the Schrödinger evolution. The holonomy is therefore a genuine $*$-unitary, and the cycle-averaged phase for each eigen-component is the corresponding diagonal entry, while the off-diagonal entries rotate the superpositions. 53 The Bell Basis as the Idempotent Basis of B⊗B $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ - Maximal entanglement is a continuum, not a finite list. 58 The Bloch Ball as the Normalised Slice of the Future Light Cone $\exp(2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ So it is not merely that the two groups differ: the boost factor is what a Lorentz transformation has and a $*$-unitary does not, and the central phase is what a $*$-unitary has and a boost does not. The twisted spinor article records the division on the other action: of the two, the unit-norm case is the Lorentz transformation on the material sector, while the $*$-unitary case is a rotation with no dilatation.

70 The Current Algebra $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$, $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ Every bilinear runs at twice the field's phase, and the factor of two is where the interference frequency comes from. 71 The Current Algebra $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ The norm is invariant under both frames and it is the only bilinear that sees the phase. The boost and the rotor are units of natural norm one, so they cancel; what remains is $r^{2}e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$.
72 The Current Algebra $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$, $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ The phase enters doubled, and the range of the phase is a consequence. The field carries $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ and the norm carries $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$. Two consequences follow and both are used below: the modulus $\rho$ is unchanged by a shift of the phase through a half turn, and the norm's argument traverses a full turn while the phase traverses a half turn. The range $\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}\in(-\tfrac{\pi}{2},\tfrac{\pi}{2}]$ that the polar articles use is not a convention chosen for convenience: it is the range that makes the map from the phase to the norm's argument a bijection.
73 The Current Algebra $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ The word is $\tilde\Xi=r\,e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}\tilde U_{\Psi,\mathrm b}\tilde u_{\mathrm r}$ with $r>0$ real, the phase central, the boost Hermitian positive of unit natural norm and the rotor real unitary. The Relativistic Quantum Space as a Fluid states the word and its eight parameters; this article takes it and asks what a quadratic product of the field can see.
74 The Current Algebra $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ Proof. The current is the density times the boost's inverse square by the polar article's proposition, and the spin is the density times the rotor's image of the declared axis, likewise. 79 The Dirac Equation in Biquaternionic Polar Variables $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ The factor of two is the whole of zitterbewegung. The trembling of a state of this kind is a bilinear effect: the density and the current oscillate, and every bilinear is built from the norm, whose angle is twice the field's. The field's phase advances at the rest rate $mc^{2}/\hbar$, so its square advances at $2mc^{2}/\hbar$, and that is the trembling frequency; the doubling is one line of algebra, $\langle\tilde\Xi\rangle_{\natural}=\rho e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$, and not a property of the flow. The numbers are the standard ones: $1.55269\times10^{21}\ \mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}$ for the rest frequency and $193.1\ \mathrm{fm}$ for half the reduced Compton wavelength.

81 The Dirac Equation in Biquaternionic Polar Variables $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ The two norms are separated and the density is identified. $\langle\tilde\Xi\rangle_{\natural}=\rho e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ and $\langle\tilde\Xi\rangle_{*}=\gamma\rho$ are two different objects, and for a central field they coincide. The identification of $\rho$ as the modulus of the quaternionic norm, and of the Hermitian norm as its time-dilated image, is a statement about which quantity a density is built from.
82 The Dirac Equation in Biquaternionic Polar Variables $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ Why this is not the nodal set. For a central field the two sets coincide: with $B^{2}=0$ identically, $\langle\tilde\Xi\rangle_{\natural}=A^{2}e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ and the norm vanishes exactly where the amplitude does. Here they separate, and the separation is the whole difference between the scalar companion and this article. A one-sided plane wave is nowhere zero and has $N=0$ everywhere, so the word fails on a field of uniform, nonzero density. The failure is a failure of the chart's range and not of the field's size.
83 The Dirac Equation in Biquaternionic Polar Variables $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ Proof. The quaternion conjugation $\natural$ acts on the $\mathbb{H}$ factor, so it leaves the central $i$ alone, inverts the real rotor and inverts the Hermitian boost. The rotor and the boost cancel against their own inverses, the central part squares, and $\langle\tilde\Xi\rangle_{\natural}=r^{2}e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$. The star conjugation is the coefficient conjugation composed with $\natural$; it fixes a Hermitian factor, conjugates the central $i$ and inverts the rotor, so the phases cancel, the rotors cancel, and $\tilde\Xi\tilde\Xi^{*}=r^{2}\tilde U_{\Psi,\mathrm b}^{2}$. Squaring the boost gives $\cosh\theta_{\mathrm{rpd}}\,e_t+i\sinh\theta_{\mathrm{rpd}}\,\vec u_{\mathrm b}$, whose scalar part is $\cosh\theta_{\mathrm{rpd}}$.

116 The Field-Strength Biquaternion and Its Invariants $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ because the conjugate negates the vector part of each coefficient and the electric part is the imaginary vector while the magnetic part is the real vector. Read on the fields this is $\vec E\to-\vec E$ and $\vec H\to\vec H$: the conjugate reverses the electric field and keeps the magnetic one. The duality rotation of the later section, $\tilde{F}\mapsto \exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})\tilde{F}$, turns the electric planes into the magnetic ones and back. At $\theta_{\mathrm{pha}} = \pi/2$ the factor is $-i$, which carries $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$ to $\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$ and back, so the two families are exchanged: with unit impedance the exchange is the exact swap $\vec E\mapsto\vec H$, $\vec H\mapsto-\vec E$, and in a general medium the impedance factor $Z=\sqrt{\mu/\epsilon}$ enters. This is why the duality rotation is a symmetry of the source-free vacuum equations and not of the equations with electric sources: it moves the field along a circle in the $(I_1,\,2cI_2)$-plane, at constant $I_1^2+4c^2I_2^2$, without preserving the planes separately.

126 The Klein–Gordon Equation in Biquaternionic Polar Variables $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})$ A varying frame is not available. The algebra's polar word applies to any $N$-regular element, so $\tilde\Xi=r\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}})\tilde U_{\Psi,\mathrm b}\tilde u_{\mathrm r}$ exists for a general field; but the frame rotor and the rotor are then fields, their derivatives do not vanish, and substituting the word into the Klein–Gordon equation gives eight equations for the eight real parameters, with the derivatives of the frame entering to the same order as those of the amplitude. The term appears only because the operator is second order: a first-order operator applied to $r e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ returns one derivative of the amplitude and no curvature, which is why the polar form of the Dirac wave stops at the eikonal and the four-momentum (Relativistic Mechanics from the Dirac Wave).

132 The Klein–Gordon Equation in Biquaternionic Polar Variables $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ The word is the boxed form of the introduction. 164 The Pauli Correspondence and the Matrix Image $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$, $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ The central element $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}e_t$ maps to the scalar matrix $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}\mathrm I_2$. 190 The Polar Form of the Dirac Equation in Biquaternionic Form $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$, $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ The factor of two in the angle is not decoration. The field carries $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ and the norm carries $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$, so every bilinear built from the norm runs at twice the rate of the field's own phase. Two consequences follow and both are used below: the density is insensitive to the sign of $\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}$, and a superposition of two branches beats at twice the difference of their angles.
191 The Polar Form of the Dirac Equation in Biquaternionic Form $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ The two norms are separated and the density is identified. $\langle\tilde\Xi\rangle_{\natural}=\rho e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ and $\langle\tilde\Xi\rangle_{*}=\gamma\rho$ are two different objects, and for a central field they coincide. The identification of $\rho$ as the modulus of the quaternionic norm, and of the Hermitian norm as its time-dilated image, is a statement about which quantity a density is built from.
192 The Polar Form of the Dirac Equation in Biquaternionic Form $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ Proof. The quaternion conjugation $\natural$ acts on the $\mathbb{H}$ factor, so it leaves the central $i$ alone, inverts the real rotor and inverts the Hermitian boost. The rotor and the boost cancel against their own inverses, the central part squares, and $\langle\tilde\Xi\rangle_{\natural}=r^{2}e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$. The star conjugation is the coefficient conjugation composed with $\natural$; it fixes a Hermitian factor, conjugates the central $i$ and inverts the rotor, so the phases cancel, the rotors cancel, and $\tilde\Xi\tilde\Xi^{*}=r^{2}\tilde U_{\Psi,\mathrm b}^{2}$. Squaring the boost gives $\cosh\theta_{\mathrm{rpd}}\,e_t+i\sinh\theta_{\mathrm{rpd}}\,\vec u_{\mathrm b}$, whose scalar part is $\cosh\theta_{\mathrm{rpd}}$.

195 The Polar Form of the Dirac Equation in Biquaternionic Form $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ The polar form of the Dirac field of this corpus is $\tilde\Xi=r\,e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}\tilde U_{\Psi,\mathrm b}\tilde u_{\mathrm r}$ with $r>0$ the scale, $\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}$ the central angle, $\tilde U_{\Psi,\mathrm b}$ a Hermitian positive boost and $\tilde u_{\mathrm r}$ a real rotor. The parameters are eight and the components are eight, so the word is a bijection, and its domain is the $N$-regular set: the polar form exists exactly where $N\neq0$ and fails on every $N$-degenerate field.
196 The Polar Form of the Dirac Equation in Biquaternionic Form $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ The two norms separate. The quaternionic norm is $\langle\tilde\Xi\rangle_{\natural}=\rho e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ and the Hermitian norm is $\langle\tilde\Xi\rangle_{*}=\gamma\rho$, the difference being that the star conjugation fixes the Hermitian boost while the natural conjugation inverts it and leaves the central $i$ alone. The density is the modulus of the first and it is the Lorentz scalar; the Hermitian norm is its time-dilated image. The norm's argument is twice the word's angle, and the doubling is the factor of two behind zitterbewegung.

214 The Schrödinger Equation in Biquaternionic Form $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ and this expression is precisely where the conflation happens. The $i$ in $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ is the central scalar imaginary, and the angle $\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}$ is a number; the rotor $\tilde u_{\mathrm r}$ is built from a non-central root $\vec u$, and $\vec u$ is an axis the field carries. One is the complex structure, the other a direction the field has turned, and they must be read separately.
215 The Schrödinger Equation in Biquaternionic Form $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}$ so $i$ carries $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$ out of itself; it is not an operator on $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$. The phrase "the complex structure of $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$", if used at all, can only mean the complex structure of the carrier: as a real vector space $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$ can be given some complex structure abstractly, but none is canonical, and multiplication by $i$ is not one. A reader is most likely to slip here, because the polar word $r\,e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{pha}}}\tilde U_{\Psi,\mathrm b}\tilde u_{\mathrm r}$ contains both an $i$, inside the central factor, and a non-central root inside the rotor; the $i$ does not act on $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$, it maps it to its complement.

1 A Brief History of Biquaternions in Physics $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ - Kazuhiko Nishijima, "On the theory of leptons," Il Nuovo Cimento 5 (1957) 1349–1354, for the two-parameter group $G_N=\sigma \exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})+\bar{\sigma}\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}})$ of the doubled system, where $\theta_{\mathrm{cen}}$ and $\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}$ are the two real angles of the central and axial halves. the phases of the historical versor notation
2 Algebra and Element Conventions $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ The central phase is carried by the first coordinate. The phase is the unit complex number $\lambda=\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ multiplying $e_t$, and it acts on an element by left multiplication, $\tilde Q\mapsto\lambda e_t\tilde Q=\lambda\tilde Q$. On the coefficients this is $Q_t\mapsto\lambda Q_t$ and, with the same $\lambda$, $Q_x\mapsto\lambda Q_x$, $Q_y\mapsto\lambda Q_y$, $Q_z\mapsto\lambda Q_z$. The first line is the definition: the phase acts on $Q_t$, the coefficient of $e_t$, the first coordinate of the element, and there it rotates the two parameters $q_t$ and $q'_t$ of the centre $\mathbb{B}_{C}=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_t,ie_t\}$. The other three coordinates follow, with the same $\lambda$, because the multiplication is $\mathbb{C}$-linear and $e_t$ is the unit. The action is therefore fixed by its value on one coordinate, and there is no second choice to make. The element carries one complex structure, $J=L_{ie_t}$, of which the phase is the exponential, $\lambda e_t=\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}} J)$; a second complex structure exists in the double $\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{B}_{\mathbb{R}}$ and in no element of $\mathbb{B}$. the versor convention: a generator times an angle
3 Anomalies and Anomaly Cancellation in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ The abelian factor. For the central $\mathrm{U}(1)$ the coefficient is the cubic charge sum. The framework's own central phase acts as a scalar on the algebra: multiplication by $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ multiplies every coefficient of a state by the same factor, so a state and its partner of the opposite chirality carry the same charge $ϙ$. The two contributions to the cubic sum are therefore $+ϙ^3$ and $-ϙ^3$, the sign being the left-minus-right rule and not a difference of charges,

The phase is central in $\mathbb{B}$, so it multiplies every element alike, and it therefore commutes with the chirality basis operator of the frame, the multiplication on the right by $-i\vec e_z$. The basis operator is not itself central; it is what separates the two parts named by the subscript. What matters here is only that the central phase commutes with it, which gives that phase the same factor on the two parts. 14 Bosonization in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ - The mass term is the exchange of the two values; in the arena it is $m\overline{\tilde{Ξ}}\vec e_y$, the exchange being carried by the right factor $\vec e_y$ because the conjugation fixes the real chirality. 15 Bosonization in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ 2. The framework's mass is what bosonization requires, and its carrier is a right factor. The series convention for the mass does not multiply the field by a coefficient: it is written on the other side of the element, $m\Gamma\overline{\tilde{Ξ}}$ (The Dirac Equation in Biquaternionic Form). It therefore pairs the two values of the label: a central scalar multiplies both values by the same number and stays diagonal, while the right factor of the mass is one of the units that exchange the values. In two dimensions that unit is $\vec e_y$, and the exchange is the whole of the mass's structure. In two dimensions this is the standard Dirac mass, whose bosonized image is the cosine of the sine-Gordon model. The framework's mass term therefore maps to the bosonic interaction without a change of form, and the phase of the mass term — the framework's central phase $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ multiplying the mass — maps to a shift of the boson field, which is the standard statement that the fermion mass's phase is the bosonic field's vacuum angle. The equivalence is thus exact at the level of the mass term, and the framework's mass convention is compatible with it rather than competing with it.
16 Bosonization in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ The phase is therefore a shift of the boson field. In the framework the mass's phase is a central phase $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$, the same object as the $\theta_{\mathrm{vac}}$ vacuum's weighting of The Theta Vacuum in Biquaternionic Form, and the shift it induces is the two-dimensional instance of the statement that a $\theta_{\mathrm{cen}}$ angle is a bosonic vacuum angle. The framework's statement is therefore exact and structural: the central phase of the mass is the central phase of the vacuum, and bosonization realises it as a field shift.
17 Bosonization in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ and the framework's contribution is to name the two sides: the fermion is an element of the algebra, read in one value for the count, and the boson is a central scalar, so that the equivalence is a correspondence between the algebra's operator algebra and the centre's exponentiated algebra. The counting $c_{\text{Dirac}}=2\times\tfrac12=1=c_{\text{boson}}$ matches the central charges, the framework's linear mass — the exchange of the two values, carried in the arena by the right factor $\vec e_y$ — bosonizes to the cosine, and the mass's central phase $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ bosonizes to a shift of the boson field, the same central phase that weights the $\theta_{\mathrm{vac}}$ vacuum. The dictionary, the sine-Gordon equivalence, the Schwinger model, and the non-abelian case with its Wess–Zumino term are standard and transcribed.
18 Bosonization in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ with the chirality inserted beside the timelike basis operator. The two insertions act on opposite values — the chirality keeps the value and the timelike basis operator exchanges it — and the scalar part of the resulting element is imaginary, so $i$ times it is real; the check is a recomputation on generic elements. A chiral rotation by $\theta_{\mathrm{cen}}$ rotates the two densities into one another, and a mass $m=\|m\|\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ selects the direction $\theta_{\mathrm{cen}}$ in that plane. In the arena the coefficient conjugation preserves each value, so the two densities are real with respect to it and the rotation is a real rotation; its bosonized image is the real, doubly-periodic shifted cosine of the previous paragraph, not a phase. In four dimensions the coefficient conjugation reverses the chirality and therefore exchanges the values, so a real element pairs a value with the conjugate of the other and the two values are not two independent real planes; the arena's cosine image has no four-dimensional analogue. A chiral rotation still exists in four dimensions, but it is anomalous and becomes a $\theta_{\mathrm{vac}}$ term rather than a shift of a boson. This is the algebraic face of the counting failure recorded below, and it was verified on the actions.

112 The Neutron in Biquaternionic Form $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}}}$ A price is paid. The two parameters are independent on the superpositions, but the common mass links the two Lanczos fields. 144 The Standard Model under the Biquaternion Framework — A Research Agenda $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$, $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ The centre gives one charge, not two. The framework's own gauge group, drawn from the centre, is vector-like: a central element multiplies every element by the same scalar, so the two parts receive the same charge, and it cannot be a chiral gauge group. (The article records a qualification: under the alternative real-structure reading the central phase would act axially, with $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ on the $L$ part and $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ on the $R$ part, and which reading is physical is not settled. Under neither reading does the centre supply independent left and right charges, so a derived chiral gauge group remains out of reach either way.)

146 The Standard Model under the Biquaternion Framework — A Research Agenda $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ Two properties of this factor are as important for the agenda as the factor itself. First, the charge $ϙ$ is a parameter; the algebra does not fix it. Second, a central element acts as a scalar on the algebra: left multiplication by $\lambda = \exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ multiplies every element by the same factor. A gauge group drawn from the centre therefore assigns every element the same charge — it is vector-like and cannot distinguish left from right. This is established in Chiral Fermions in the Biquaternion Framework, and it is the reason the abelian factor as it stands cannot be weak hypercharge, which is chiral.

148 The Two Sectors: Split and the Four Bridges $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ The precise statement is the superselection interpretation of the split. The algebra is central simple, so there is no central projection that would split it into two ideals. What survives is a real-structure grading carried by $\flat=-{}^{*}$: the sectors are its eigenspaces, no congruence carries one into the other, and the only relative phase the algebra offers is the central phase $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$, which acts as a scalar on the module and is invisible on every ray. Because the conjugation action preserves the sectors, no congruence converts material data into informational data or the reverse, and there is no observable coherence between the two. The module action, left multiplication, does mix the sectors, and it is not an operation in the physical sense.

$\theta_{\mathrm{pwp}}$

The angle is the plane-wave phase, $\vec k_\Phi\cdot\vec r-\omega r'_t/c$. It is real because the wave vector, the position, the frequency and the interval are real.

10 Canonical Quantization of the Biquaternion Dirac Field $e^{+i\theta_{\mathrm{pwp}}}$, $e^{-i\theta_{\mathrm{pwp}}}$ the four terms grouping as $e^{+i\theta_{\mathrm{pwp}}}$ times the action with $+mc$ and $e^{-i\theta_{\mathrm{pwp}}}$ times the action with $-mc$. Each piece now has a definite parity in $\vec p$: the time part of the action contributes $i\frac{E_{\vec p}}{c}(\tilde{Ξ} i\vec e_x)\vec e_x=\frac{E_{\vec p}}{c}\tilde{Ξ}$, because $i\vec e_x\cdot i\vec e_x=i^2\vec e_x^2=e_t$, and it is even, since $E_{\vec p}$ and the units are; the spatial part $-i\sum_\eta p_\eta\vec e_\eta(\tilde{Ξ} i\vec e_x)\vec e_y$ and the mass term $2im\sin\theta_{\mathrm{pwp}}(\tilde{Ξ} i\vec e_x)$ are odd. The even part is $2E_{\vec p}\cos\theta_{\mathrm{pwp}}$ times the identity and, after division by $2E_{\vec p}$, integrates to $\delta^{(3)}(\vec r_{12})$; the odd parts vanish under symmetric integration. Hence the equal-time relation holds. The cancellation of the odd terms is exactly what the opposite phases of the two branches accomplish; had both phases been equal, the cross terms would have survived and the canonical relation would have failed.

50 Explicit Solutions of the Biquaternionic Dirac Equation $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{pwp}})$ Why the elementary field is null. 59 Klein–Gordon from the Dirac Square in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{pwp}})$, $\exp(+i\theta_{\mathrm{pwp}})$ The first-order equation selects half of that space, and its solution is fixed by one half alone. Each of the eight modes is annihilated by $\Box - k_m^{2}$, because that operator acts on each coefficient separately and the plane wave is its eigenfunction, but the converse fails: a general second-order solution of the pair is a combination of sixteen independent modes with arbitrary coefficients, and the two members cut that space down to eight by relating the halves at each frequency. The square therefore has a kernel twice as large as the pair's; it is a necessary condition, not a sufficient one.

$\theta_{\mathrm{act}}$

The angle is the action over $\hbar$. It is real because the action of a real configuration is real, and the real part is taken before dividing.

# Article The form The line as written What the exponent carries
1 Algebra and Element Conventions $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ The split is also a split of roles, and the two roles belong to the two factors of the tensor product and cannot be exchanged. The central unit $i$ is the scalar imaginary: it is what enters the phase $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$, and it multiplies a whole element from inside the centre. The three units $\vec e_x,\vec e_y,\vec e_z$ are the vector directions: they are the relativistic and rotational structure, and they enter through the quaternion factor. A central scalar and a vector direction are different kinds of object — one commutes with everything, the other rotates — so no product of the four general products may read $i$ through the quaternion direction or a unit through the scalar one. The mirror statement, that the two conjugations act on the two factors separately, is the subject of The Four General Products: Composition, Probability, Causality, Gauge.

3 Each Sector Is the Other's Clock: the Sector Exchange as Relational Time $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ Throughout this article $\theta_{\mathrm{act}}:=\mathrm{Re}\,S/\hbar$ is the angle of the action: it is real whenever the action is real, and it is the angle the wave form carries. 10 The Action Principle and the Classical Limit in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ - The stationary-phase lemma evaluates an oscillatory sum over trajectories as a sum over the critical points of the action, weighted by $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ and the fluctuation factor. 11 The Action Principle and the Classical Limit in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ Two statements are joined in this article. The first is the action principle: the physical trajectory of a classical system is the trajectory at which the action $S=\int \mathrm{Lag}\,dt'$ is stationary, and the stationarity condition is the Euler–Lagrange equation. The second is the classical limit as stationary phase: when a sum over trajectories is weighted by the phase $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$, the sum is dominated, as $\hbar\to0$, by the trajectories near the stationary ones, so that the classical paths are exactly the critical points of the action. The first statement is the classical theory; the second is the statement that the classical theory is the critical-point theory of an oscillatory integral.
12 The Algebrodynamical Programme: Nonlinear Cauchy–Riemann Conditions, Self-Quantized Charge, and Induced Causal Geometry $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ The printed chain cannot hold as written, and the de Broglie endpoint decides which half of it is meant. The two halves disagree by a factor two: with the free-particle action $A=Mc\int dS$ and $S$ the Minkowski interval, the condition $\frac{\delta A}{\hbar}=2\pi N$ gives $\delta\alpha=(\frac{c\,M}{\hbar})\int dS$, not $(\frac{2\,c\,M}{\hbar})$ times it. Recomputed here, with $S=\sqrt{{r'_{t}}^2-L^2}$ for a path of length $L$ flown in a time $r'_t$ at speed $V=L/r'_t$, the derivative is $\partial S/\partial L=-V/(c\sqrt{1-V^2/c^2})$, so the fusion spacing at $\mathrm{Const}=\frac{2\,c\,M}{\hbar}$ is $\lambda=h\sqrt{1-V^2/c^2}/(2MV)$, half the de Broglie wavelength, and at $\mathrm{Const}=\frac{c\,M}{\hbar}$ it is $\lambda=h\sqrt{1-V^2/c^2}/(MV)$ exactly, the de Broglie wavelength, with non-relativistic limit $h/(MV)$. The corpus records the de Broglie relation as the source's claim and the factor as unreconciled: the printed $\mathrm{Const}=(\lambda_0/2)^{-1}$ and the printed $=\frac{\delta A}{\hbar}$ cannot both be meant, and the chronon reading — a quantum of complex time of Compton order and not of Planck order — survives either way. The source notes its correspondence with Feynman's $\tilde{Ξ}=R\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$, whose phase is proportional to the classical action — for a free particle, to the proper time — and, expanding $dS$ in powers of $V/c$ and using the integrability of the zeroth-order term, recovers the ordinary de Broglie relation

21 The Functional Integral in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ There is a third kind of object in Dirac's extension of the construction to fields, and it is the one that plays the role the particle kernel plays rather than the role $Z$ or $Z[\tilde J_{\Phi}]$ plays. If $S[\tilde{Ξ}]$ is the integral of the Lagrangian density over a region of spacetime, the quantum analogue of $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ is a generalized transformation function: a functional of the field values on the boundary of the region, obeying the same composition law as the kernel — split the region in two, multiply the two functionals, and integrate over the field values on the common boundary. Applied repeatedly, the law connects a region with its infinitesimal sub-regions and contains the quantum analogue of the action principle for fields. Unlike the particle kernel of the companion article, it is not in general a transformation between two sets of dynamical variables, and its probability interpretation is correspondingly restricted: the square of its modulus is a relative a priori probability only when the region is bounded by two separate three-dimensional surfaces, each extending to infinity in the space directions and lying entirely outside every light cone with vertex on the other. That condition is the field-theoretic counterpart of the causal ordering that the Feynman propagator's $i\epsilon$ encodes — the amplitude between two configurations is defined when neither surface reaches the other — and it is a restriction on the region and not on the algebra. The object is formal, a limit of the same finite-dimensional integrals as $Z$; it is named here for what it is, a boundary-value functional rather than a number. the weight, with no further structure

23 The Functional Integral in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ Two consequences of the distinction matter for the framework. 25 The Gauge Field Path Integral in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ The functional integral is the series' general apparatus for the quantum theory of a field, and its general properties — the sum over field configurations weighted by $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$, the generating functional, the effective action — are treated in the subcategory devoted to them and are not repeated here. The present article does one thing: it specializes the functional integral to the gauge field. That specialization is not a matter of substituting one field for another, because the gauge field's functional integral is ill-defined as it stands. The action is invariant along gauge orbits, the integrand is constant along those orbits, and the integral therefore contains an infinite factor — the volume of the gauge group — for every orbit. Making the gauge-field path integral finite is the work of the Faddeev–Popov construction, and it is that construction, in the biquaternion framework, that this article develops.
26 The Gauge Field Path Integral in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ The functional integral over a gauge field is ill-defined as it stands, because the action and the measure are invariant along gauge orbits and the integral contains the infinite volume of the gauge group. 32 The Klein–Gordon Path Integral in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ The division of labour with the companion path-integral article must be stated at once. The companion article The Path Integral in Biquaternionic Form analyses the algebraic status of the phase: it shows that the $i$ of $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ is the central scalar imaginary, that the exponent lies in the material sector $\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$, that the phase is a central $*$-unitary element, and that a non-central root would produce a spin rotation rather than a global phase. This article does not repeat that analysis; it takes the centrality of the phase as established and addresses the complementary case, the relativistic field-theoretic one: a functional integral over a field configuration space, not a single-particle sum over paths, with the Klein–Gordon quadratic form in the exponent. The non-relativistic single-particle scalar case, which is a sum over paths with a central kernel, belongs to the Schrödinger setting and is not treated here.
33 The Klein–Gordon Path Integral in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$ The path integral is the third formulation of a quantum theory, alongside the equation of motion and the operator algebra. For a relativistic scalar field it is the functional integral over field configurations, weighted by the phase $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{act}})$, and it organises the whole of the free and perturbative theory: the generating functional is a Gaussian in the presence of a source, the free two-point function is the inverse of the quadratic form, the classical field is the stationary point, and the loop expansion is the expansion of the determinant about that point. This article derives the Klein–Gordon functional integral in biquaternionic form, evaluates the free Gaussian exactly, identifies its two-point function with the propagator of the companion article The Klein–Gordon Propagator and Its Green's Functions in Biquaternionic Form, and states what the algebra contributes.

A complex action carries both factors; a real action carries the angle alone.

6 Chiral Fermions in the Biquaternion Framework $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{gau}}}$ A chiral abelian gauge symmetry with independent charges is consistent. 15 Gauge Transformations of the First and Second Kind in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})$ Tanişli's EPL paper writes the source-free Maxwell equations as $\nabla F=0$ with $F=\vec E+i\vec B$, gives the first-order density $\mathrm{Lag}=F^*\cdot\nabla F$, and derives the Poynting theorem from the local transformation $F\to(1+\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})F$ with $\theta_{\mathrm{gau}}$ a function of $x$ and $r'_t$. The transformation is the corpus's duality rotation: $F$ is the Riemann–Silberstein vector in dimensionless units, and $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})F$ rotates $\vec E$ and $\vec B$ into one another, leaving $\vec E^2+\vec B^2$ invariant and rotating the pair $(I_1,2cI_2)$ by $2\theta_{\mathrm{gau}}$, checked to $8.9\times10^{-16}$. The naming is reversed against Weyl: a transformation depending on an arbitrary function is of the second kind, and the second kind yields a differential identity rather than a conservation law, which is the content of Noether's second theorem and the subject of Noether's Two Theorems. The transformation is also not a symmetry for position-dependent $\theta_{\mathrm{gau}}$: the equation $i\partial/\partial r'_t\,\vec N=c\,\mathrm{rot}\,\vec N$ acquires the extra terms $-(\partial/\partial r'_t\,\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})\vec N$ and $ic\,(\nabla\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})\times\vec N$, which do not cancel, and the residual grows from the finite-difference floor to half the field magnitude. The paper's "$F^*\cdot(\nabla F)=0$" is a contraction of the field equation, and its expansion using the curl equations is the Poynting identity — the standard consequence of the field equations, whose origin in the corpus is translation invariance by Noether's first theorem. Three internal inconsistencies of the printed algebra are recorded: the product rule against the basis rule, the sign of the imaginary part of $F$, and the sign of the second block of the expansion.
16 Gauge Transformations of the First and Second Kind in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})$ The transformation is written $(1+\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})e_tF$, which to first order is $\exp(\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})F$, and the paper identifies this with $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})F$. The two agree only when $\theta_{\mathrm{gau}}$ is purely imaginary. A real infinitesimal $\theta_{\mathrm{gau}}$ produces the common scaling $\vec E\to(1+\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})\vec E$, $\vec B\to(1+\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})\vec B$, which is also a symmetry of the source-free equations — the equations are linear and homogeneous — but which is a dilation rather than a duality rotation. The corpus's duality rotation requires the parameter that multiplies $F$ to be a phase, so the imaginary interpretation is the one that makes the paper's own identification correct.

21 Physics Units and Common Elements Conventions $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})$ Dimensionless and dimensionful. A pure number — the fine-structure constant $\alpha$, the ratio $g=2$, the phase $\frac{S}{\hbar}$, the trace normalisation $2$ — carries no unit and no conversion. An exponent is a pure number, and the coupling is what keeps it one: a phase is written $\frac{S}{\hbar}$, a holonomy is written $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})$, and the line integral is not an exponent on its own, because $\oint_C\sum_\mu\varepsilon_\mu A_\mu dx_\mu$ carries $\mathrm{Q}$ and no choice of $c$, $\hbar$ or $G$ removes it. An article that writes the exponent without the coupling declares the coupling absorbed into the connection, once, where the display is made. A dimensionful constant is a unit conversion and is written with its unit. No scale is derived by the algebra: $c$, $\hbar$, $k_B$, $G$, $m$ and $ϙ$ are inserted at the places where the structure requires one. The metrology says the same thing from the other side. The defining constants above are fixed by definition and the units are realised from them; $\alpha$ and $G$ are the ones that have to be measured, and $\alpha$ is the dimensionless one of the two. No article is permitted to set one of these constants to one: $c$, $\hbar$, $k_B$, $G$, $m$ and $ϙ$ are carried, and the reason is given in the paragraphs that follow. the units of the holonomy and of the translation phase

26 The Berry Phase and Geometric Phases in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})$ If $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})$ is single-valued on the loop, the last integral is $2\pi k$ for some integer $k$, the winding number of $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})$ around the loop. 28 The Berry Phase and Geometric Phases in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{gau}})$ The gauge-principle article derives its abelian connection from a precise algebraic object: the centre $\mathbb{B}_{C} = \mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_t, ie_t\}$ of the algebra, whose $*$-unitary part is $\mathrm{U}(1) = \{\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})\}$, with $\theta_{\mathrm{cen}}$ the real central angle. 59 The Nodal Set and Quantized Circulation $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{gau}}(r)}$ Under a gauge transformation the field multiplies by a unit central element and the winding is unchanged for a single-valued transformation. Take $\tilde\Xi\mapsto\tilde\Xi\,e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{gau}}(r)}e_t$ with $\chi$ a single-valued function. A transformation with a multivalued $\chi$ is not a gauge transformation of the field on the loop: it changes the field's value at each point and it changes $n$. So the charge is gauge invariant under the gauge group of single-valued transformations, and it classifies the field's configurations rather than being a redundant label.

25 Physics Units and Common Elements Conventions $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{pwt}})$ A frequency is a rate; a mode energy is $\hbar$ times it. The symbol $\omega_k$ is an angular frequency, an inverse time, and the energy of the mode it labels is $\hbar\omega_k$; a display that writes the energy itself as $\omega_k$ has set $\hbar=1$ at that place. Because the corpus's temporal coordinate is the length $r'_t$, the phase of a plane wave is $k\cdot x=\vec k_\Phi\cdot(r_x\,\vec e_x+r_y\,\vec e_y+r_z\,\vec e_z)-\omega_k r'_t/c$: an inverse time multiplies a length only after division by $c$, and a display that writes $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{pwt}})$ over an inverse-time $\omega_k$ has set $c=1$ in the exponent. Where an article works with a material frequency already in inverse length — the case of a mode equation $\frac{d^2u}{dr_t^{\prime 2}}+\omega^2u=0$ — the phase is $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{pwt}})$ with no $c$, the material four-wavevector is $\tilde K_\Phi=i k_{t}^{\prime}e_t+\vec k_\Phi$, and the article says so once; the two readings are never mixed inside one display, because a mixture is a factor $c$ that no longer shows.

65 Zitterbewegung in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{pwt}})$, $\exp(+i\theta_{\mathrm{pwt}})$ They are splits of different objects. 3 Exercise: Spin Precession in a Magnetic Field $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})$ 6. The result agrees with the standard formulation. This is not surprising, because the two formulations are the same mathematics in different notation: $\mathbb{B} \cong M_2(\mathbb{C})$, and the biquaternion evolution $\tilde{U}(r'_t) = \exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})$ corresponds exactly to the standard matrix evolution $T(r'_t) = \exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})$ under the isomorphism. 10 Quantum Channels and the Reversible/Irreversible Dichotomy $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})$ The dichotomy has a sharp algebraic form here. A state map is reversible if and only if it is conjugation by a single $*$-unitary biquaternion, $\tilde{Ϛ}_{\Psi} \mapsto \tilde Q\tilde{Ϛ}_{\Psi}\tilde Q^{*}$ with $\tilde Q\tilde Q^{*} = e_t$. Each one-parameter subgroup is generated by a Hamiltonian $\tilde H_\Psi \in \mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$ through the exponential $\tilde Q(r'_t) = \exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})$. The irreversible maps are the rest: the completely positive, trace-preserving maps, which are sums of conjugations rather than a single conjugation. The canonical example is dephasing, worked out below in the $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$ formulation.

12 Quantum Channels and the Reversible/Irreversible Dichotomy $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})$ The reversible/irreversible dichotomy is the dichotomy of Kraus rank. A channel is reversible if and only if it has Kraus rank one, which happens if and only if it is conjugation by a $*$-unitary biquaternion, $\tilde{Ϛ}_{\Psi} \mapsto \tilde Q\tilde{Ϛ}_{\Psi}\tilde Q^{*}$ with $\tilde Q\tilde Q^{*} = e_t$. This is exactly the class of inner automorphisms of the algebra, and exactly the class of channels that preserve purity. The one-parameter subgroups are generated by Hamiltonians $\tilde H_\Psi \in \mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$ through $\tilde Q(r'_t) = \exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})$, whose generators $-\frac{i\,H}{\hbar\,c}$ lie in the complementary subspace $\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$. A channel is irreversible if and only if its Kraus rank is at least two; the general irreversible channel is a sum of conjugations, and by Stinespring dilation it is the reduction of a reversible evolution on a larger system with the environment discarded.

23 Similitudes Between Biquaternion Rotors and Hamiltonian Flow $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})$ The rotor is required to have unit biquaternion norm, $\langle R\rangle_{\natural} = R R^{\natural} = e_t$, and this is what makes the conjugation preserve $\langle\tilde{Q}\rangle_{\natural} = \tilde{Q}\tilde{Q}^{\natural}$ and the subspace $\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$; it is also what selects the traceless generators, as shown above. It is important to keep this condition distinct from unitarity, $R R^{*} = e_t$, which is a different equation. The two coincide for real quaternions (rotations) but not in general: a boost rotor is unit-norm and Hermitian, hence not $*$-unitary; while the evolution operator of quantum physics, $\tilde Q(r'_t) = \exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})$ with a Hamiltonian $\tilde H_\Psi = h_t e_t + i\vec h_\Psi$ that has a trace part, is $*$-unitary but has
24 Similitudes Between Biquaternion Rotors and Hamiltonian Flow $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})$, $\exp(2i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})$ the first summand containing the rotations (the traceless real pure quaternions), the second the boosts (the traceless imaginary pure quaternions). In the language of the companion article on quantum physics, the Lie algebra of the $*$-unitary group is $\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$; the traceless part of $\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$ gives the compact rotations, the traceless part of $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$ the non-compact boosts. The scalar generator $i\alpha e_t$, whose phase $\theta_{\mathrm{evo}}=\alpha r\'_t$ is real because $\alpha$ and $r\'_t$ are, lies in $\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$ but is not traceless: it generates the $*$-unitary phase $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})e_t$, whose norm is $\exp(2i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})e_t \neq e_t$ and is therefore $*$-unitary but not a unit-norm rotor. The rotor group is the traceless part, not all of $\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$.

37 The Schrödinger Equation in Biquaternionic Polar Variables $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}})$ Two settings, and they are not the same equation. The second is where Madelung's pair is read, and it closes because a central field supplies one scale and one phase. $\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}$

The angle is the axial phase of one chiral half. It is real because each chiral half is a unit complex number and the angle is its argument.

1 A Brief History of Biquaternions in Physics $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}})$ - Kazuhiko Nishijima, "On the theory of leptons," Il Nuovo Cimento 5 (1957) 1349–1354, for the two-parameter group $G_N=\sigma \exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})+\bar{\sigma}\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}})$ of the doubled system, where $\theta_{\mathrm{cen}}$ and $\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}$ are the two real angles of the central and axial halves. the phases of the historical versor notation
2 Chiral Fermions in the Biquaternion Framework $e^{-i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$, $e^{+i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$ The axial rotation is off the centre. The chirality operator generates a one-parameter group by its exponential: $\tilde{Ξ}\mapsto e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}\xi}\tilde{Ξ}$, which is the right multiplication $\tilde{Ξ}\mapsto\tilde{Ξ}\,e^{\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}\vec e_z}$ (Anomalies and Anomaly Cancellation in Biquaternionic Form, where the identity is used). Its family is a family of right multiplications, and it multiplies the $L$ part by $e^{-i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$ and the $R$ part by $e^{+i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$: it is genuinely axial, and its generator $\vec e_z$ is not central, so the group it generates is not a subgroup of the centre.
3 Chiral Fermions in the Biquaternion Framework $e^{-i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$, $e^{+i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$, $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$ The vector phase and the axial rotation. Both parts carry the phase $\lambda$ under the central phase $Q\mapsto\lambda Q$, and the phases $e^{-i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$ and $e^{+i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$ under the axial rotation $Q\mapsto Qe^{\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}\vec e_z}$. The bilinear pairs one part with the other, so the two phases enter it as a product: the amplitude the mass term is built from carries the phase $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$, and the term is invariant exactly when $e^{2i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}=1$, that is for $\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}\in\pi\mathbb{Z}$, the sign $\tilde{Ξ}\mapsto-\tilde{Ξ}$. The mass term therefore conserves the vector $\mathrm{U}(1)$ and breaks the axial one; the survival of $\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}\in\pi\mathbb{Z}$ is the statement that a bilinear is even in the field. The axial current, built from $\xi$ in the companion Anomalies and Anomaly Cancellation in Biquaternionic Form, consequently has a divergence proportional to the mass bilinear and vanishes only at $m=0$; the vector current is conserved at every $m$.

9 The Chiral Condensate and Dynamical Symmetry Breaking in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(+i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}})$, $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}})$ which is the original equation only when $m=0$; the second member fails in the same way. The reason is the one the side gives, and it is worth stating plainly, because it is not a failure of commutation: every term of the pair is a left multiplication, and the rotation is a right multiplication, so the rotation commutes with the gradient and with the mass. What it does is multiply an $R$-valued element by $\exp(+i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}})$ and an $L$-valued one by $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}})$, and the mass is the only term of the theory that sets one value beside the other. A term that pairs the two values cannot survive a rotation that moves them oppositely.
10 The Fujikawa Method and the Path-Integral Anomaly in Biquaternionic Form $e^{+i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$, $e^{-i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$ The rotation is the axial symmetry of the linear chiral pair. The rotation $\tilde{Ξ}\mapsto\tilde{Ξ} e^{\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}\omega}$ rotates the parts of the two values in opposite directions, because the multiplier is $-1$ on $\tilde{Ξ}_{L}$ and $+1$ on $\tilde{Ξ}_{R}$ in the grading sense, and multiplies them by $e^{+i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$ and $e^{-i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$ in the phase sense. It is the axial phase whose breaking was treated in the companion article on the condensate; there the breaking was spontaneous and belonged to the vacuum, here it is explicit and quantum-mechanical. The two are different phenomena acting on the same symmetry, and the framework keeps them distinct: the condensate supplies a coefficient to the linear chiral pair, and the anomaly violates the axial current even when the coefficient is zero.
11 The Fujikawa Method and the Path-Integral Anomaly in Biquaternionic Form $e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$ The two rotations compared. The framework's two continuous symmetries of the massless equation behave differently in the measure, and the difference is exact. The vector rotation $\tilde{Ξ}\mapsto e^{i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}\tilde{Ξ}$ is multiplication by a central element: it rotates the two values in the same sense, and it is exactly what a gauge transformation does to the field. Because the regulator is gauge invariant, the trace its Jacobian produces is the one without the pseudoscalar, and that trace is even under the interchange of the two index pairs, so it contracts to $F_{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}$ and never to $\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}F_{\mu\nu}F_{\rho\sigma}$. An even local term is absorbed by the counterterms that define the effective action, so the divergence of the vector current is not corrected. In a chiral gauge theory the same distinction appears as the cubic charge sum $\sum_LQ^3-\sum_RQ^3$, which vanishes for a vector-like spectrum. The axial rotation inserts the pseudoscalar; it rotates the two values oppositely, its leftover is $\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}F_{\mu\nu}F_{\rho\sigma}$, and no local counterterm with the symmetries of the theory absorbs it. Only the second rotation is anomalous. In the framework's terms the mass term and the measure agree about which phase is broken: the linear chiral pair conserves the central phase and breaks the axial one at the classical level, and the measure breaks the same axial phase once more at the quantum level. The two breakings add, and their sum is the ABJ divergence.
12 The Fujikawa Method and the Path-Integral Anomaly in Biquaternionic Form $e^{+i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$, $e^{-i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$ The Fujikawa method derives the axial anomaly as the Jacobian of the fermion measure under a chiral rotation. In the biquaternion algebra the massless equation is one first-order equation in one field, $\tilde\nabla_\Phi\tilde{Ξ}=0$, the massive equation is the chiral pair $\tilde\nabla_\Phi\tilde{Ξ}_{R}=k_m\tilde{Ξ}_{L}$, $(\tilde\nabla_\Phi)^{\natural}\tilde{Ξ}_{L}=k_m\tilde{Ξ}_{R}$ — because $\tilde\nabla^2$ is not scalar while $\tilde\nabla_\Phi(\tilde\nabla_\Phi)^{\natural}=\Box_\Phi$ is — and the chiral rotation is the right multiplication $\tilde{Ξ}\mapsto\tilde{Ξ} e^{\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}\omega}$ by the pseudoscalar $\omega=-\vec e_z$. The two values are the two eigenspaces of right multiplication by $\vec e_z$, of complex dimension two each, and the rotation multiplies them by $e^{+i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$ and $e^{-i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}}$. The local rotation adds the axial-current term; the measure does not add it, and the Jacobian is $(\det U)^{-2}=\exp(-2i\int\theta_{\mathrm{axr}} a)$, with the regulated trace $a=\lim_{M\to\infty}\mathrm{tr}[\rho_\omega e^{-\mathcal{H}/M^2}]$ as the index density, the insertion taken on the right, and $A=2a$ as the anomaly density.

The physical content is the pair of statements $\gamma_- - \gamma_+ = \theta_{\mathrm{sol}}$ and $\gamma_\pm$ defined modulo $2\pi$; stating either phase as a single absolute real number requires choosing a patch, and the choice is not physical. This is the first trap of the introduction, made concrete.

6 The Klein–Gordon Equation in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{rst}})$ The central structural question has a negative answer. The second-order structure does not fit the $\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}/\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$ split naturally. Since $\Box$ is central, the split decouples the equation into two sectors hosting two copies of the same real solution space, exchanged by multiplication by $i$; the $\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$ part of a solution is the $i$-image of an $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$ solution, so the pairing is the real/imaginary-part split of a complex field, not a conjugate pair. The conjugate pair is complex conjugation, which preserves both sectors, and the doubling the equation actually needs is the spinor-module doubling of the first-order square root $\tilde\nabla_\Phi$, whose mass term is the linear coupling of the two chiralities. That doubling is representation-theoretic, not the real fixed-point decomposition. The non-relativistic limit is the general one of The Three Wavenumbers of a Particle, applied to this field, and it needs one preliminary step: the rest-mass phase $f = \exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{rst}})$, which is itself the zero-momentum solution, is divided out, and the mass term then cancels exactly. The limit is then the leading term of an expansion of $k_t' = \sqrt{k_\Phi^{2}+k_m^{2}} = k_m+\Omega$, with $\Omega = k_\Phi^{2}/2k_m$, so that $\hbar c\,\Omega = p^{2}/2m$; the term dropped is $p^{4}/(8m^{3}c^{2})$, and the ratio of dropped to kept is $k_\Phi^{2}/(4k_m^{2}) = v^{2}/4c^{2}$. What remains is the parent article's free Schrödinger equation, $i\hbar\,\frac{\partial\chi}{\partial t'} = -\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\Delta_\Phi\chi$, with the same central scalar imaginary $i$; the two articles use one complex structure and no competing unit, and no second-order remnant is left behind.

The answer worked out below is that it requires a doubling, and that the doubling is not $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}\oplus\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$; the sector split gives two $i$-related copies of the same equation, not the particle–antiparticle pair. $\theta_{\mathrm{any}}$

The angle is the exchange angle of a braid. It is real because a one-dimensional representation is a unit complex number.

# Article The form The line as written What the exponent carries
1 Anyons and Braid Statistics in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ 5. Anyons and the two-sector mixing. The exchange phase $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ with arbitrary $\theta_{\mathrm{any}}$ is a continuous interpolation between the bosonic and fermionic phases. Is there a biquaternion object — an element interpolating between a real and an imaginary quaternion — whose continuous parameter is the statistics angle, and does the interpolation pass through the material and informational sectors in a definite order?
2 Anyons and Braid Statistics in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ In three or more spatial dimensions the exchange of two identical particles is described by the symmetric group: exchanging them twice returns the wavefunction to itself, and the only possibilities are the two one-dimensional representations, bosons and fermions. In two spatial dimensions this conclusion fails, because the worldlines of two particles can wind around one another without colliding, and the topology of the configuration space is that of the braid group rather than the symmetric group. The braid group admits one-dimensional representations labelled by a continuous angle, and particles whose exchange produces a phase $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ for arbitrary $\theta_{\mathrm{any}}$ are anyons. This article develops the anyon and the braid statistics in the biquaternion framework, the last case of the higher-spin subcategory: the one that lies outside the integer-or-half-integer classification that the preceding articles used.
3 Anyons and Braid Statistics in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ In two spatial dimensions the exchange of identical particles is described by the braid group $B_n$ rather than by the symmetric group $S_n$; the braid generators satisfy $\sigma_i\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_i=\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_i\sigma_{i+1}$ and do not square to the identity, and the symmetric group is the quotient in which they do. The one-dimensional $*$-unitary representations are $\sigma_i\mapsto \exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ for arbitrary $\theta_{\mathrm{any}}$, giving anyons; the higher-dimensional representations give non-abelian anyons. These statements were verified: the permutation matrices of $S_3$ satisfy the braid relation and square to the identity; the reduced Burau representation satisfies the braid relation without squaring to the identity; and the one-dimensional representation satisfies it for every angle. The one-dimensional representations factor through the abelianization $B_{\mathrm{ab},n}\cong\mathbb{Z}$, so that the abelian anyon's phase is the coordinate on $\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{U}(1))=\mathrm{U}(1)$; the level-$k$ representations of the $\mathrm{SU}(2)$ type factor through the Temperley–Lieb algebra with $d=2\cos(\pi/(k+2))$, which gives $d=\tfrac{1+\sqrt5}{2}$ at $k=3$; and the Fibonacci quantum dimension satisfies $d_\tau^2=1+d_\tau$, whose solution $d_\tau=(1+\sqrt5)/2$ was verified to machine precision.
4 Anyons and Braid Statistics in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ The flux–charge composite realizes the anyon, with the exchange phase $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})=\exp(\frac{i\,e\,Ξ}{2\,\hbar})$ derived from the Aharonov–Bohm effect, and the spin–statistics relation $\theta_{\mathrm{any}}=2\pi s$ ties the angle to a spin that need not be an integer or half-integer. The Chern–Simons theory supplies the field theory of fractional statistics: the loop correlator is the exponential of the linking number, the statistics angle is $\theta_{\mathrm{any}}=\pi ϙ^2/k$, and the spin is $ϙ^2/2k$, with the semion at $k=2,ϙ=1$ having angle $\pi/2$ and spin $\tfrac14$. The linking number was computed for the Hopf link by the Gauss integral and found to be $\pm1$. The non-abelian case replaces the phase by a matrix, with the fusion rules $a\times b=\sum_c N_{abc} c$ and the quantum dimension organizing the state space.
5 Anyons and Braid Statistics in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ The one-dimensional $*$-unitary representations of $B_n$ are labelled by a single angle: $\sigma_i\mapsto \exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ for any real $\theta_{\mathrm{any}}$, since the braid relations involve only products of the generators and any scalar commutes. The three familiar cases are $\theta_{\mathrm{any}}=0$ (bosons), $\theta_{\mathrm{any}}=\pi$ (fermions), and any other $\theta_{\mathrm{any}}$ (anyons). The higher-dimensional $*$-unitary representations give non-abelian anyons: the exchange is a $*$-unitary matrix rather than a phase, the matrices do not commute, and the state of a collection of such particles is a vector in a representation space rather than a single amplitude.
6 Anyons and Braid Statistics in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ \boxed{\;\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}}) = \exp(\frac{i\,e\,Ξ}{2\,\hbar}) ,
7 Anyons and Braid Statistics in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ satisfies the braid relation for general $t$ — verified at a real and at a complex value of $t$ — but not $\sigma_i^2=I$ unless $t$ takes special values, so that it is a genuine representation of the braid group and not of the symmetric group. And the one-dimensional representation $\sigma_i\mapsto \exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ satisfies the braid relation identically for every $\theta_{\mathrm{any}}$, which is the algebraic statement that the statistics angle is a free parameter in $2+1$ dimensions.
8 Anyons and Braid Statistics in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ with the image of every generator $\sigma_i$ being the same generator of $\mathbb{Z}$. A one-dimensional $*$-unitary representation is a homomorphism into an abelian group, hence factors through the abelianization, hence is determined by the image of the single generator, hence is $\sigma_i\mapsto \exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{any}})$ for some $\theta_{\mathrm{any}}$: the abelian anyon's phase is the coordinate on the dual of $\mathbb{Z}$, that is, on the circle $\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Z},\mathrm{U}(1))=\mathrm{U}(1)$. The integer that labels the abelianization is the total winding of the braid — the number of times the strands wrap, counted with sign — and the phase is its exponential. A non-abelian representation does not factor through the abelianization; it uses the full braid group, and its dimension is the dimension of the representation space. The distinction is therefore not a technicality but the algebraic content of the abelian/non-abelian division: one-dimensional representations see only the winding, and higher-dimensional ones see the braid.

Exponents That Multiply the Angle by a Factor

The exponent is $i\theta_{\mathrm{xxx}}$ multiplied by one further object: a generator, a charge, a field or a volume. The angle is still real, and the object after it carries the rest. These rows stand apart from the tables above because the argument is not the angle alone.

1 Algebra and Element Conventions $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}} J)$ An angle is real, and it is written $\theta$. The central phase is $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}} J)$, so the $i$ belongs to the exponential and not to the angle. The glyph $\theta$ carries a real number, and a subscript names which angle it is: $\theta_{\mathrm{rpd}}$ the rapidity, $\theta_{\mathrm{rat}}$ the rotation angle of an evolution. One glyph, one case, and no second letter spent on the imaginary reading.
2 Algebra and Element Conventions $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}} J)$ The central phase is carried by the first coordinate. The phase is the unit complex number $\lambda=\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})$ multiplying $e_t$, and it acts on an element by left multiplication, $\tilde Q\mapsto\lambda e_t\tilde Q=\lambda\tilde Q$. On the coefficients this is $Q_t\mapsto\lambda Q_t$ and, with the same $\lambda$, $Q_x\mapsto\lambda Q_x$, $Q_y\mapsto\lambda Q_y$, $Q_z\mapsto\lambda Q_z$. The first line is the definition: the phase acts on $Q_t$, the coefficient of $e_t$, the first coordinate of the element, and there it rotates the two parameters $q_t$ and $q'_t$ of the centre $\mathbb{B}_{C}=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_t,ie_t\}$. The other three coordinates follow, with the same $\lambda$, because the multiplication is $\mathbb{C}$-linear and $e_t$ is the unit. The action is therefore fixed by its value on one coordinate, and there is no second choice to make. The element carries one complex structure, $J=L_{ie_t}$, of which the phase is the exponential, $\lambda e_t=\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}} J)$; a second complex structure exists in the double $\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{B}_{\mathbb{R}}$ and in no element of $\mathbb{B}$.
3 Algebra and Element Conventions $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}} J)$ The central phase is the real argument. $\lambda e_t=\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}} J)$ with $\theta$ real and $J=L_{ie_t}$: the phase turns the centre by a real angle, and the rotor of a rotation is the same argument at half the angle, $\tilde u_{\mathrm r}=\cos(\theta/2)e_t+\sin(\theta/2)\vec u_{\mathrm r}$.

The two agree. The passage between the two pictures is the standard one: the Hermitian observables $\tilde S_{\Psi,\eta}$ generate the $*$-unitary rotations through $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{rat}}\,\sum_\eta \frac{\vec u_\eta\,\tilde{S}_{\Psi,\eta}}{\hbar})$, and the anti-Hermitian generators $g_\eta$ are the elements of the Lie algebra.

7 Bosonization in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}}\xi_{\mathrm{scl}})$ The framework's interpretation of a vertex. The operator $:\!\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}}\xi_{\mathrm{scl}})\!:$ is built from the exponential of a central field: $\xi_{\mathrm{scl}}$ is central, so the vertex operator is the exponential of a central element, and it is the only kind of local operator the bosonic side has. The fermion on the other side is an anticommuting element of the algebra. The equivalence therefore identifies a central exponential with an anticommuting element, and the identification is not an isomorphism of the two algebras as they stand: the boson's field commutes with itself and the fermion's anticommutes, and the anticommutation is the statistics statement, carried on the bosonic side by the cocycle of the vertex algebra, whose coefficient is the same anomaly as before. Read this way, the bosonization dictionary is a map between a commuting object and an anticommuting one, made an isomorphism of operator algebras by the vertex algebra's central extension. This is the algebraic content of the equivalence and it is worth stating plainly, because it shows the dictionary's non-triviality: an associative central exponential and an anticommuting element are not the same object, and only the extension makes them equivalent.

16 Goldstone's Theorem in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}}_0)$ The scalar sector's canonical continuous symmetry is the central phase $\mathrm{U}(1)$ generated by the Noether charge of the current $\tilde J_{\Phi}\in\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$, acting on the central-valued field by $\xi_{\mathrm{fop}}\to \exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})\xi_{\mathrm{fop}}$. 19 Norm and Pairing Conventions $e^{-i\theta_{\mathrm{rpd}}\vec u_{\mathrm b}}$ Linear and bilinear. The word stores the boost in the frame rotor and deposits it twice in the four-vector; its parameter is $\theta_{\mathrm{rpd},w}=2\theta_{\mathrm{rpd}}$.

32 Squeezing Beyond the Biquaternion Algebra $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{rat}} \vec e_\mu)$ In the framework those generators are exactly the ones that produce the spin structure: $\mathsf{M}_2(i\vec e_\mu)=-i\sigma_\eta$, and a rotation $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{rat}} \vec e_\mu)$ acts on the module as a spin rotation. The framework's statement is sharp: the operator that squeezes the module is a rotation of the module, and the sibling subcategories treat it. The companion article Angular Momentum and Spin in Biquaternionic Form develops the rotation generators; the spin-squeezing problem itself is the subject of a spin subcategory article.

36 The Chiral Condensate and Dynamical Symmetry Breaking in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(2i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}\xi)$ Why both are symmetries. The two writings are kept apart throughout: the pair is the one-sided statement in the algebra, and the two-sided equation $(\mathcal{D}-k_m)\tilde{Ξ}=0$ with $\mathcal{D}\tilde{Ξ}=-\sum_\mu e_\mu(\partial_\mu\tilde{Ξ})\vec e_{\nu(\mu)}$ is the writing in which the bilinears are read. Chirality is a side and not a sign carried by an element: $\xi=-iR_{\vec e_z}$ is the frame's fifth basis operator and multiplies on the right, the gradient multiplies on the left, and the mass is the one term of the equation with both slots and therefore no side. The massless two-sided equation carries both the vector and the axial phase, $\tilde{Ξ}\mapsto\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{cen}})\tilde{Ξ}$ and $\tilde{Ξ}\mapsto\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}\xi)\tilde{Ξ}=\tilde{Ξ}\exp(\theta_{\mathrm{axr}}\vec e_z)$; the mass term preserves the central one and breaks the axial one, with $\sum_\mu\partial j_{5\mu}/\partial x_\mu=2m\,\tilde{Ξ}^*iR_{\mathrm{axf},\vec e_x}(\tilde{Ξ}\vec e_z)$. A nonzero condensate reproduces exactly this breaking, spontaneously, and its value is the coefficient of the same linear chiral pair: $m=-2G\langle\tilde{Ξ}^*iR_{\mathrm{axf},\vec e_x}\tilde{Ξ}\rangle$. The order parameter sits on the real central line, and it is not the antilinear pairing built on the algebra's real structure $\flat$.

79 The Partition Function in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}}\tilde H_\Psi)$ This is a genuine modification, but it is not forced by the framework: observables are defined to be Hermitian elements of $\mathbb{B}_{\Psi}$, and a Hermitian $\tilde H_\Psi$ is the case treated in the body of the article. The non-Hermitian case is what one gets if one allows the generator to have an $\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$ component, and it connects to two standard ideas without deriving either: complex temperature (continuing $\beta\to\beta+i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}}$, which turns the trace into $\mathrm{Tr}(\exp(-\beta H)\exp(-i\theta_{\mathrm{evo}}\tilde H_\Psi))$ and exposes real-time evolution), and the imaginary chemical potential used in finite-density lattice studies, where an imaginary parameter again converts a sign problem into a phase. Whether an $\mathbb{B}_{\Phi}$-valued generator is physically meaningful in this framework, and whether the resulting complex free energy has an interpretation beyond these known techniques, is open.

95 The Theta Parameter and Strong CP in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{vac}} Ϙ)$ Parity. Under parity $\vec E\mapsto-\vec E$ and $\vec B\mapsto+\vec B$, and under time reversal $\vec E\mapsto+\vec E$ and $\vec B\mapsto-\vec B$, so $I_2=\vec E\cdot\vec B\mapsto-I_2$ in both cases. Under either transformation the theta action changes sign, $S_\theta\mapsto-S_\theta$. A term linear in a parity-odd density with a real coefficient $\theta_{\mathrm{vac}}$ is therefore not invariant under parity unless $\theta_{\mathrm{vac}}=0$ or $\theta_{\mathrm{vac}}=\pi$ (mod $2\pi$), where the theta weight $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{vac}} Ϙ)$ is real for every integer $Ϙ$. For generic $\theta_{\mathrm{vac}}$ the theory violates $P$ and $T$, and by CPT it violates $CP$. The statement is purely algebraic in the abelian sector: the framework has one parity-even and one parity-odd derivative-free invariant, and the theta term is the integral of the odd one.
96 The Theta Vacuum in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{vac}} Ϙ)$ Periodicity. The phase is periodic, $\exp(i(\theta_{\mathrm{vac}}+2\pi)Ϙ)=\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{vac}} Ϙ)$ for integer $Ϙ$, so the physical parameter space is a circle of circumference $2\pi$ and only $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{vac}})$ (and, with fermions, more refined combinations) is physical. In the framework this is the statement that the phase is an element of the central circle $\mathbb{B}_{C}$'s unit subgroup, and that the circle has the period of the central imaginary; there is nothing further to derive, because the periodicity is the periodicity of $\exp(i\cdot)$. The one refinement worth recording is that with a chiral fermion whose determinant also carries a phase, the physically invariant combination is $\bar\theta_{\mathrm{vac}}=\theta_{\mathrm{vac}}+\arg\det M$, the sum of the gauge angle and the fermion determinant's phase; the framework's division of labour makes the origin of the second term explicit, since it is the determinant phase of the previous section.

106 The Theta Vacuum in Biquaternionic Form $\exp(i\theta_{\mathrm{vac}} Ϙ)$ its abelian density is $\mathrm{Sc}(\tilde F\star\tilde F)=I_2=\vec E\cdot\vec B$, and the dual obeys $\star^2=-1$; the instanton article supplies the configurations and the integrality, inherited here. The framework therefore supplies the angle's value space — its centre — and shows that the $\theta_{\mathrm{vac}}$ vacuum does not disturb the sector structure. What Came From the Inventory

Two rows of that file are fully patched and were kept: the one that states the boost factor in the section on real exponents, and the one that records a checked spelling.
